=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given sequences of positive integers A and B of length N. Process Q queries given in the following forms in the order they are given. Each query is of one of the following three types.

- 
Type 1: Given in the form 1 i x. Replace A_i with x.

- 
Type 2: Given in the form 2 i x. Replace B_i with x.

- 
Type 3: Given in the form 3 l r. Solve the following problem and print the answer.

- 
Initially, set v = 0. For i = l, l+1, ..., r in this order, replace v with either v + A_i or v \times B_i. Find the maximum possible value of v at the end.




It is guaranteed that the answers to the given type 3 queries are at most 10^{18}.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \cdots A_N
B_1 B_2 \cdots B_N
Q
query_1
query_2
\vdots
query_Q

Here, query_i is the i-th query, given in one of the following formats:
1 i x

2 i x

3 l r

Output

Let q be the number of type 3 queries. Print q lines. The i-th line should contain the answer to the i-th type 3 query.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq B_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq Q \leq 10^5
- For type 1 and 2 queries, 1 \leq i \leq N.
- For type 1 and 2 queries, 1 \leq x \leq 10^9.
- For type 3 queries, 1 \leq l \leq r \leq N.
- For type 3 queries, the value to be printed is at most 10^{18}.

Sample Input 1

3
3 2 4
1 2 2
3
3 1 3
1 1 1
3 1 3

Sample Output 1

12
7

For the first query, the answer is ((0 + A_1) \times B_2) \times B_3 = 12.
For the third query, the answer is ((0 + A_1) + A_2) + A_3 = 7.

Sample Input 2

6
65 32 12 5 8 312
4 1 3 15 16 2
6
3 2 6
3 1 5
1 5 6
2 4 9
3 2 6
3 3 5

Sample Output 2

46080
69840
27648
1728

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Can we compute envelope of a block in O(S) using the breakpoint composition property? Let's derive.

We have sequence of functions f_i. We want F(v) = f_n ∘ ... ∘ f_1(v) (left to right). Each f_i has breakpoint t_i = a_i/(b_i-1) if b_i>1; if b_i=1, f_i(v)=v+a_i (no breakpoint, slope 1). Composition F is strictly increasing PL. We can compute its breakpoints and slopes maybe by processing from right to left? There is a known algorithm for composing "max of two lines" functions: maintain the envelope as a list of intervals [x_k, x_{k+1}] with affine expression s_k v + c_k. To compose with a new function f (on the right? or left), we need update.

If we build block from left to right: F_new = f_i ∘ F_old. f_i has breakpoint y0. The new breakpoints are old breakpoints plus v0 where F_old(v0)=y0. Since F_old is strictly increasing, we can find v0 by binary search on envelope. Then update lines:
For v <= v0, F_old(v) <= y0, so f_i chooses add: F_new(v)=F_old(v)+a_i.
For v >= v0, F_old(v) >= y0, so f_i chooses multiply: F_new(v)=b_i * F_old(v).
Thus F_new is simply F_old plus a_i on left, and b_i times F_old on right. This is easy! Wait is that correct? f_i(y)=max(y+a_i, b_i y). The threshold y0 satisfies y0+a_i = b_i y0 => y0 = a_i/(b_i-1) if b_i>1. If F_old(v) <= y0, add branch; if >= y0, multiply branch. Since F_old strictly increasing, there is a unique v0. Therefore composing on the left (adding f_i after old segment) transforms the function by:
F_new(v) = F_old(v) + a_i for v <= v0,
F_new(v) = b_i * F_old(v) for v >= v0.
This is a simple "bend" at v0. Great! This means we can maintain envelope in O(number of pieces) by splitting at v0 and applying affine transformations to left/right. But applying to all pieces naively O(size) per composition. Maybe we can precompute for each block a data structure to evaluate F_block(v) in O(log S) using the breakpoint list. Building this data structure after update can be done in O(S) if we use the composition-by-bend property and maintain pieces with a stack? Let's investigate O(S) build.

We need compute F for a sequence of length S. Process left to right: start F(v)=v (piece slope1 intercept0, no breakpoints). For each f_i with threshold y_i = a_i/(b_i-1) (if b_i>1; if b_i=1, F_new=F+a_i, no split, just add to all intercepts? F_new(v) = F(v)+a_i for v <= v0
F_new(v) = b_i * F(v) for v >= v0.
Note at v0 both equal.

This operation is exactly: split the domain at v0, add a_i to the left part's intercept, multiply the right part's slope and intercept by b_i. If v0 lies inside a piece, split that piece into two. This adds at most one breakpoint. We need maintain pieces as intervals [x_k, x_{k+1}] with affine L_k(v)=s_k v + c_k. To apply operation:
- Find piece containing v0.
- Split at v0.
- For all pieces left of v0: c += a_i (slope unchanged).
- For all pieces right of v0: s *= b_i, c *= b_i.
This is a range affine update on a list of pieces. We can implement with a treap where each node is a piece, supports split by value v0, lazy add to prefix, lazy multiply to suffix, and search by value. How to compose two envelopes in O(size_L + size_R)? There is a standard algorithm for composing PL functions when both are strictly increasing and represented as breakpoints.

Let's define representation of a small function F on [0, INF]:
- Breakpoints x_0=0 < x_1 < ... < x_m = INF? Actually domain [0, INF], pieces intervals [x_j, x_{j+1}] with affine L_j(v)=s_j v + c_j. Since F is strictly increasing and convex, slopes s_j increasing, intercepts c_j decreasing. We can store breakpoints x_j and for each piece slope s_j, intercept c_j, and maybe value at breakpoints. To evaluate F(v), find interval containing v, return s_j*v+c_j.

For composition H = F_R(F_L(v)). We can compute breakpoints of H:
- Breakpoints of F_L: x such that F_L has kink.
- Preimages under F_L of breakpoints of F_R: since F_L strictly increasing, for each breakpoint y of F_R, there is at most one x with F_L(x)=y. These x become breakpoints of H.
Thus H's breakpoints are union of F_L breakpoints and F_L^{-1}(F_R breakpoints). Since F_L is strictly increasing, we can merge two sorted lists:
  - F_L breakpoints sorted by x.
  - For each F_R breakpoint y, compute x = F_L^{-1}(y) using F_L envelope; these x are sorted in same order as y because F_L increasing.
Then H pieces are obtained by composing the affine functions on each interval. We can compute slopes/intercepts for H by evaluating? Need derive.

If on an interval of x, F_L(v)=s v + c, and F_R on the corresponding y-interval is S y + C. Then H(v)=S(s v + c)+C = (S s) v + (S c + C). So we can build H pieces by pairing intervals.

Let's derive composition algorithm with saturation.

Thus sat_H = min(sat_L, first_v_where_L >= sat_R).

Let's implement composition efficiently.

First, define helper functions:

- eval_piece? For composition, we need:

We can implement a function first_ge(L, y) that returns smallest integer v in [0, sat_L] such that L(v) >= y. Since L strictly increasing on unsaturated part. If y <= L(0), return 0. If y > L(sat_L-1) (max value before saturation), return sat_L (meaning no v < sat_L; at v=sat_L L=INF >= y). But if sat_L=0 handled. We need L(sat_L-1) maybe if sat_L>0. Since L is increasing, max unsaturated value at v=sat_L-1. We can compute by evaluating last piece at sat_L-1.
If y <= L(0): return 0.
If y > max_val: return sat_L.
Else binary search over integer v? Domain up to INF, but pieces small. We can find piece by scanning starts and solving.

Because pieces count <=60, we can simply scan pieces to find where y falls:
For each piece i with interval [starts[i], starts[i+1]-1] (last [starts[-1], sat_L-1]):
  val_start = L(starts[i]) (maybe compute)
  val_end = L(end) (end = next_start-1 or sat_L-1)
  If y <= val_end, then the first v is within this piece. Since L(v)=s*v+c increasing. Solve s*v+c >= y => v >= ceil((y-c)/s). Clamp to starts[i]. Return v.
If y > val_end, continue.
Because pieces are sorted and increasing, O(m) per query. For composition, we need first_ge for sat_R and for each R start (up to 60). O(m*n) if scan each. But m,n<=60, 3600 per merge maybe too high. We can optimize by two-pointer or binary search.

Since m<=60, scanning for each R start O(60*60)=3600 per merge; 1.7e6 merges ->6e9 too high. Need O(m+n).

We can compute preimages of all R starts in O(m+n) by merging because both L starts and R starts sorted. Let's design.

We have L pieces sorted by v. For a given y, first_ge can be found by binary search on breakpoints? We can precompute for each L piece the value range [val_start, val_end]. Since pieces sorted, values increasing. Then first_ge(y) can be found by binary search over pieces (bisect on val_end). m<=60, bisect in C? We can use bisect_left on a list of val_ends. Then solve within piece. That's O(log m) per y. For n<=60, O(n log m) ~360 operations per merge, 1.7e6 ->600M bisect calls maybe high but maybe okay? Need reduce.

Alternatively, since R starts are increasing, we can compute first_ge for each R start by walking through L pieces once:
- Maintain current L piece index p.
- For each R start y in increasing order:
  - Advance p until y <= val_end[p] (or y > max_val -> return sat_L).
  - Compute v = ceil((y - c[p]) / s[p]), clamp to start[p].
This is O(m+n) because p only increases. Great.

We also need sat_H = min(sat_L, first_ge(L, sat_R)). We can compute first_ge for sat_R using same walk.

Then build H starts:
- We need include all L starts < sat_H.
- Include preimages of R starts (starts_R[k] for k>=1, starts_R[k] < sat_R) that are < sat_H. Since R starts sorted increasing, their preimages under L are increasing. We can compute them by walking L pieces as above.

Then merge two sorted lists of starts: L_starts (filtered < sat_H) and preimages (filtered < sat_H). Since both sorted and start with 0. We can merge with two pointers.

But we also need for each H interval the L index and R index. We can determine during merge:
- L index: current L piece index. Since H starts include all L starts, between H starts no L breakpoint, so L index constant. We can maintain by advancing while next L start <= current H start? Actually at a given v, L index = index of piece containing v. We can update as we process starts.
- R index: R piece containing y=L(v). Since H starts include preimages of R starts, between H starts no R breakpoint, so R index constant. We can maintain by advancing while next R start <= y.

Simpler: While generating H starts, we can also generate for each interval the L and R indices by simulating.

Let's define L:
startsL length mL, slopesL, interceptsL, satL.
For v < satL, piece index p_L(v) = max j with startsL[j] <= v (since starts sorted, starts[0]=0). Because startsL are starts of pieces; piece j valid until startsL[j+1]-1 or satL-1.

R similarly.

To compute H pieces:
We will have H_starts sorted, length mH. For each interval [H_starts[k], H_starts[k+1]-1] (last to satH-1), we need:
- L_piece index i: the piece of L valid on this interval. Since H_starts includes all L starts, i is constant. We can find i as the index of the L piece that contains H_starts[k].
- R_piece index j: piece of R valid for y=L(v). Since H_starts includes preimages of R starts, j is constant. We can find j as index of R piece containing y = L(H_starts[k]) (or any v in interval).

Then H slope = slopesR[j] * slopesL[i]; H intercept = slopesR[j] * interceptsL[i] + interceptsR[j].

Need ensure H value < INF for v<satH. Since satH chosen, yes.

Implementation plan for compose(L,R):

Inputs: L and R are tuples (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts). For sat=0, starts empty.

If L.sat == 0 or R.sat == 0:
  return sat=0, starts=[], slopes=[], intercepts=[]  # constant INF
  (Because R(INF)=INF; if L sat=0, L(v)=INF for all v. If R sat=0, R(y)=INF for all y>=1; L(0)>=1 so H=INF. Need confirm if L.sat>0 but L(0) could be 0? Not for nonempty. We'll assume. To be safe, if R.sat==0 and L(0)==0, H(0)=R(0) maybe <INF, but no nonempty segment has L(0)=0. We can ignore or handle by evaluating? Better not need identity.)

If L.sat > 0 and R.sat > 0:

Compute maxL = L(satL-1) (value at last unsaturated integer). Since satL could be INF+1? We'll set satL as integer 0..INF+1. If satL=INF+1, last unsaturated v=INF, maxL = L(INF) < INF? But for nonempty segment, L(INF) > INF, so satL would never be INF+1? Actually if A small and B=1, L(v)=v+A, at v=INF, L=INF+A > INF, so sat=INF-A <= INF. So satL <= INF always. Because F(v)>v, at v=INF, F(INF)>INF, so saturation occurs at or before INF. Thus satL <= INF. Good. For nonempty segment, satL in [0, INF]. So last unsaturated v = satL-1 if satL>0.

Compute v_R = first_ge(L, R.sat). This is smallest v with L(v) >= R.sat. Since R.sat <= INF. If R.sat <= L(0), v_R=0. If R.sat > maxL, v_R=satL (because at v=satL, L=INF >= R.sat). Then satH = min(satL, v_R). Note if v_R=0, satH=0 (H saturates at 0). That means H(0)>=INF. Good.

Pseudo:
def first_ge_many(L, ys):
    # ys sorted increasing
    res=[]
    p=0
    mL=len(startsL)
    # precompute ends and val_ends maybe
    for y in ys:
        if y <= interceptsL[0]:  # L(0)
            res.append(0)
            continue
        # advance p while p < mL and val_ends[p] < y
        while p < mL and val_ends[p] < y:
            p += 1
        if p >= mL:
            # y > maxL
            res.append(satL)
        else:
            # compute v
            s=slopesL[p]; c=interceptsL[p]
            # ceil((y-c)/s)
            num = y - c
            v = (num + s - 1)//s
            if v < startsL[p]: v = startsL[p]  # should not
            # also ensure v <= end_p? Since val_ends[p]>=y, solve gives <= end_p.
            res.append(v)
    return res

But careful: p should not decrease between calls. Since ys increasing, val_ends[p] < y for current y; previous p may be such that val_ends[p] >= previous y. For next larger y, if val_ends[p] >= y, no advance; good. If val_ends[p] < y, advance. This works.

Function make_leaf(A,B):
INF = 10**18
if B == 1:
    # F(v)=v+A
    sat = INF - A  # because at v=INF-A, value=INF. If A>=INF? A<=1e9 so sat>0.
    # But if sat < 0? no.
    starts=[0]; slopes=[1]; intercepts=[A]
    return (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts)
else:
    start_mult = (A + (B-2)) // (B-1)  # ceil(A/(B-1))
    # sat = min(INF - A, ceil(INF / B))
    sat_add = INF - A
    sat_mul = (INF + B - 1) // B
    sat = sat_add if sat_add < sat_mul else sat_mul
    # Build pieces for v < sat.
    starts=[]; slopes=[]; intercepts=[]
    # add piece for v in [0, min(start_mult, sat)-1] if start_mult > 0 and sat > 0
    if sat > 0:
        if start_mult > 0:
            starts.append(0); slopes.append(1); intercepts.append(A)
        # multiply piece starts at start_mult, if start_mult < sat
        if start_mult < sat:
            starts.append(start_mult); slopes.append(B); intercepts.append(0)
        # If start_mult >= sat, only add piece covers all unsaturated v.
        # If start_mult == 0 impossible.
    return (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts)

If start_mult < sat, two pieces. At v=start_mult, multiply piece value = B*start_mult. Is it equal to add? Since start_mult=ceil(A/(B-1)), at v=start_mult, add = start_mult + A, multiply = B*start_mult. Which is larger? For start_mult = ceil(A/(B-1)), we have start_mult >= A/(B-1), so B*start_mult >= start_mult + A. So multiply branch is >= add. Good. For eval_node:
def eval_node(node, v):
    sat, starts, slopes, intercepts = node
    if v >= sat:
        return INF
    # find piece: idx = bisect_right(starts, v) - 1
    idx = bisect_right(starts, v) - 1
    res = slopes[idx]*v + intercepts[idx]
    if res > INF: return INF
    return res

Range query [l,r) 0-indexed:
Need process nodes in left-to-right order. Standard:
```
l += size; r += size
left_nodes = []
right_nodes = []
while l < r:
    if l & 1:
        left_nodes.append(tree[l]); l += 1
    if r & 1:
        r -= 1
        right_nodes.append(tree[r])
    l //= 2; r //= 2
nodes = left_nodes + right_nodes[::-1]
v = 0
for node in nodes:
    v = eval_node(node, v)
    if v >= INF:
        # Since answer guaranteed <= INF, if v==INF we can break? If v>INF cap.
        v = INF
        # But if v==INF, further operations keep INF; break okay.
        # However if v==INF and final answer guaranteed <=INF, it must be exactly INF and no need continue.
        break
print(v)
```

Let's write compose carefully.

```
INF = 10**18
CONST_INF = (0, (), (), ())

def compose(L, R):
    if L is None: return R
    if R is None: return L
    satL, startsL, slopesL, interceptsL = L
    satR, startsR, slopesR, interceptsR = R
    if satL == 0 or satR == 0:
        return CONST_INF
    mL = len(startsL)
    # compute ends and val_ends
    # To avoid creating lists every compose? mL small, but many merges. Need optimize.
```
Creating endsL and val_endsL lists each compose adds overhead. We can compute val_ends on the fly in first_ge loops? Since we need val_ends for while comparisons. We can precompute val_endsL as a list. mL<=61, okay. But 1.7e6 merges * list creation maybe overhead. We can optimize by storing val_ends in node? But node currently doesn't include. We could include val_ends in node tuple to avoid recomputing. Since node immutable, we can store (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, ends, val_ends). But memory more. Or compute val_ends each compose; 1.7e6*60 ~100M operations, okay maybe.

We also need L0 = interceptsL[0] (since startsL[0]=0). For satL>0, startsL nonempty.

First_ge loop over startsR[1:]. startsR is tuple; slicing creates tuple. Avoid slicing by iterating over range(1, len(startsR)).

Pseudo compose with manual first_ge:

```
def compose(L, R):
    if L is None: return R
    if R is None: return L
    satL, startsL, slopesL, interceptsL = L
    satR, startsR, slopesR, interceptsR = R
    if satL == 0 or satR == 0:
        return CONST_INF
    mL = len(startsL)
    # val_ends
    endsL = [0]*mL
    val_endsL = [0]*mL
    for i in range(mL-1):
        endsL[i] = startsL[i+1] - 1
    endsL[mL-1] = satL - 1
    for i in range(mL):
        val_endsL[i] = slopesL[i] * endsL[i] + interceptsL[i]
    L0 = interceptsL[0]
    p = 0
    pre = []
    nR = len(startsR)
    # process startsR[1..nR-1]
    for idx in range(1, nR):
        y = startsR[idx]
        if y <= L0:
            pre.append(0)
        else:
            while p < mL and val_endsL[p] < y:
                p += 1
            if p >= mL:
                pre.append(satL)
            else:
                s = slopesL[p]; c = interceptsL[p]
                # s > 0 because satL>1? If satL==1 and y>L0, p>=mL, so not here.
                v = (y - c + s - 1) // s
                if v < startsL[p]:
                    v = startsL[p]
                pre.append(v)
    # compute vR for satR
    y = satR
    if y <= L0:
        vR = 0
    else:
        while p < mL and val_endsL[p] < y:
            p += 1
        if p >= mL:
            vR = satL
        else:
            s = slopesL[p]; c = interceptsL[p]
            vR = (y - c + s - 1) // s
            if vR < startsL[p]:
                vR = startsL[p]
    satH = satL if satL < vR else vR
    if satH == 0:
        return CONST_INF
    # filter L starts
    Lf = []
    for x in startsL:
        if x < satH:
            Lf.append(x)
        else:
            break
    # filter pre
    pref = []
    for v in pre:
        if v < satH:
            pref.append(v)
    # merge
    H_starts = []
    i = j = 0
    lenLf = len(Lf); lenPref = len(pref)
    while i < lenLf or j < lenPref:
        if j >= lenPref or (i < lenLf and Lf[i] < pref[j]):
            x = Lf[i]; i += 1
        elif i >= lenLf or pref[j] < Lf[i]:
            x = pref[j]; j += 1
        else:
            x = Lf[i]; i += 1; j += 1
        if not H_starts or H_starts[-1] != x:
            H_starts.append(x)
    # build H pieces
    H_slopes = []
    H_intercepts = []
    iL = 0
    iR = 0
    # We need Lf indices correspond to original startsL. Since Lf is prefix, iL index in Lf equals original index.
    for v in H_starts:
        while iL + 1 < lenLf and Lf[iL+1] <= v:
            iL += 1
        y = slopesL[iL] * v + interceptsL[iL]
        while iR + 1 < nR and startsR[iR+1] <= y:
            iR += 1
        H_slopes.append(slopesR[iR] * slopesL[iL])
        H_intercepts.append(slopesR[iR] * interceptsL[iL] + interceptsR[iR])
    return (satH, tuple(H_starts), tuple(H_slopes), tuple(H_intercepts))
```

Need check if H_starts could be empty? satH>0, Lf includes 0, so H_starts not empty.

Potential bug: In first_ge loop, p is advanced while processing startsR. If y <= L0, we append 0 but do not advance p. For next y > L0, p may be 0 and while advances. Good. If y > L0 and p advanced to some piece, for next y larger, p remains. Good.

But what if y <= L0 for some early starts, then later y > L0, p=0, while advances. Good.

What if val_endsL[p] < y and p advances; if p reaches mL, append satL. For subsequent y, p remains mL, append satL. Good.

Now, if satL==1, mL=1, endsL[0]=0, val_endsL[0]=interceptsL[0]=L0. For y > L0, while p<1 and val_ends[0]<y -> p=1, append satL=1. For y <= L0, append0. Good. vR similarly.

Now, in building H pieces, for L with satL=1 and Lf=[0], iL=0, slopesL[0]=0, y=intercept. H_slope may be 0. Good.

Need ensure H_slopes can be 0; eval handles.

Now, is satH computed correctly when vR = satL? satH=satL. If vR = satL, that means L(v)<satR for all v<satL, so R doesn't saturate before L. H saturates at satL. Good.

If vR < satL, satH=vR. At v=vR, L(v)>=satR, H(v)>=INF. For v<vR, L(v)<satR, H<INF. Good.

Now, what if R.sat is less than L(0)? vR=0, satH=0. H(0)>=INF. Correct.

def make_leaf(a,b):
    if b == 1:
        sat = INF - a
        return (sat, (0,), (1,), (a,))
    else:
        start_mult = (a + b - 2) // (b - 1)
        sat_add = INF - a
        sat_mul = (INF + b - 1) // b
        sat = sat_add if sat_add < sat_mul else sat_mul
        starts = [0]
        slopes = [1]
        intercepts = [a]
        if start_mult < sat:
            starts.append(start_mult)
            slopes.append(b)
            intercepts.append(0)
        # if start_mult >= sat, only add piece.
        # But what if start_mult == 0? no.
        return (sat, tuple(starts), tuple(slopes), tuple(intercepts))
```

Now, compose uses tuples. Need import bisect? We don't use bisect in compose if manual. Eval uses bisect_right. We'll import bisect.

Eval:
```
from bisect import bisect_right
def eval_node(node, v):
    if node is None:
        return v
    sat, starts, slopes, intercepts = node
    if v >= sat:
        return INF
    idx = bisect_right(starts, v) - 1
    res = slopes[idx] * v + intercepts[idx]
    if res >= INF:
        return INF
    return res
```
If sat=0, v>=0 true, return INF. Good.

But memory increases by one tuple per node. Maybe okay? It also simplifies. For leaf, val_ends can be computed. For compose, we need val_endsL. We can store it. But for eval, not needed. Memory: val_ends length same as pieces, additional 1.6M ints ~45MB. Might be okay but maybe not necessary. Time vs memory. Recomputing val_ends in compose: 1.7e6 * up to 60 =100M multiplications/additions, significant. Storing val_ends could halve time. Let's store val_ends in node to speed compose. make_leaf:
```
def make_leaf(a,b):
    if b == 1:
        sat = INF - a
        starts = (0,)
        slopes = (1,)
        intercepts = (a,)
        val_ends = (INF - 1,)  # because (sat-1)+a = INF-1
        return (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, val_ends)
    else:
        start_mult = (a + b - 2) // (b - 1)
        sat_add = INF - a
        sat_mul = (INF + b - 1) // b
        sat = sat_add if sat_add < sat_mul else sat_mul
        starts_list = [0]
        slopes_list = [1]
        intercepts_list = [a]
        if start_mult < sat:
            starts_list.append(start_mult)
            slopes_list.append(b)
            intercepts_list.append(0)
        # If start_mult >= sat, only add piece. But if start_mult == sat? At v=sat, multiply would be active and >=INF; for v<sat add. Good.
        starts = tuple(starts_list); slopes=tuple(slopes_list); intercepts=tuple(intercepts_list)
        val_ends_list=[]
        m=len(starts)
        for i in range(m):
            if i+1 < m:
                end = starts[i+1]-1
            else:
                end = sat-1
            val_ends_list.append(slopes[i]*end + intercepts[i])
        return (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, tuple(val_ends_list))
```
Need if start_mult == 0? no. If start_mult < sat false, starts only [0]. But what if sat=0? no.

For b>1 and start_mult < sat, val_ends[0] = slopes[0]*(start_mult-1)+a = start_mult-1+a. val_ends[1] = b*(sat-1). Good.

What about L breakpoints: L changes at integer startsL[j]. We include them. Good.

- Compute val_endsH O(m+n).

- In compose, avoid `if L is None` and `if R is None` checks for internal nodes that are never None except padding. But many internal nodes with padding may be None? Let's write compose with local variables to reduce global lookups.

```
def compose(L, R):
    if L is None:
        return R
    if R is None:
        return L
    satL, startsL, slopesL, interceptsL, val_endsL = L
    satR, startsR, slopesR, interceptsR, val_endsR = R
    if satL == 0 or satR == 0:
        return CONST_INF
    mL = len(startsL)
    L0 = interceptsL[0]
    p = 0
    pre = []
    nR = len(startsR)
    for idx in range(1, nR):
        y = startsR[idx]
        if y <= L0:
            pre.append(0)
        else:
            while p < mL and val_endsL[p] < y:
                p += 1
            if p >= mL:
                pre.append(satL)
            else:
                s = slopesL[p]
                c = interceptsL[p]
                v = (y - c + s - 1) // s
                if v < startsL[p]:
                    v = startsL[p]
                pre.append(v)
    y = satR
    if y <= L0:
        vR = 0
    else:
        while p < mL and val_endsL[p] < y:
            p += 1
        if p >= mL:
            vR = satL
        else:
            s = slopesL[p]
            c = interceptsL[p]
            vR = (y - c + s - 1) // s
            if vR < startsL[p]:
                vR = startsL[p]
    satH = satL if satL < vR else vR
    if satH == 0:
        return CONST_INF
    Lf = []
    for x in startsL:
        if x < satH:
            Lf.append(x)
        else:
            break
    pref = []
    for v in pre:
        if v < satH:
            pref.append(v)
    H_starts = []
    i = j = 0
    lenLf = len(Lf)
    lenPref = len(pref)
    while i < lenLf or j < lenPref:
        if j >= lenPref or (i < lenLf and Lf[i] < pref[j]):
            x = Lf[i]; i += 1
        elif i >= lenLf or pref[j] < Lf[i]:
            x = pref[j]; j += 1
        else:
            x = Lf[i]; i += 1; j += 1
        if not H_starts or H_starts[-1] != x:
            H_starts.append(x)
    H_slopes = []
    H_intercepts = []
    iL = 0
    iR = 0
    for v in H_starts:
        while iL + 1 < lenLf and Lf[iL+1] <= v:
            iL += 1
        y = slopesL[iL] * v + interceptsL[iL]
        while iR + 1 < nR and startsR[iR+1] <= y:
            iR += 1
        H_slopes.append(slopesR[iR] * slopesL[iL])
        H_intercepts.append(slopesR[iR] * interceptsL[iL] + interceptsR[iR])
    mH = len(H_starts)
    val_endsH = []
    for idx in range(mH):
        if idx + 1 < mH:
            end = H_starts[idx+1] - 1
        else:
            end = satH - 1
        val_endsH.append(H_slopes[idx] * end + H_intercepts[idx])
    return (satH, tuple(H_starts), tuple(H_slopes), tuple(H_intercepts), tuple(val_endsH))
```

Since y > val_end previous >= val_start current? Actually val_start current = val_end previous (continuous) maybe less than y. So v computed >= startsL[p]. The clamp is safe.

Now, if `s` is 0 in first_ge division? This can happen if satL=1 and y>L0, but then p would advance to mL because val_endsL[0]=L0 < y, so no division. If satL>1, slopes positive. Good.

Now, in building H pieces, if Lf is a prefix of startsL, but startsL is tuple; Lf list. iL index in Lf equals original index. However, if startsL has a start equal to 0 and maybe another start 0? no. Good.

Now, if H_starts contains a value that is less than Lf[iL+1] but greater than Lf[iL], iL remains. If H_starts contains an L start, iL advances. Good.

Now, if H_starts contains an R preimage that is less than the first L start after 0, iL=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if R startsR has length 1 (no breakpoints). Then pre empty. vR computed. H_starts = Lf. H pieces use R index 0 for all. H_slope = slopesR[0]*slopesL[iL], intercept = slopesR[0]*interceptsL[iL]+interceptsR[0]. This is composition with affine R. Good.

Now, code for `eval_node`:
```
def eval_node(node, v):
    if node is None:
        return v
    sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, _ = node
    if v >= sat:
        return INF
    idx = bisect_right(starts, v) - 1
    res = slopes[idx] * v + intercepts[idx]
    if res >= INF:
        return INF
    return res
```
If sat=0, v>=0 true, return INF. If sat=INF? identity not stored. If v=INF and sat=INF, v>=sat true, return INF. Good.

For any node, F is nondecreasing and F(v)>v for real nodes. We store sat = min{v | F(v)>=INF}. For v<sat, F(v)<INF and is PL with integer breakpoints starts. The active affine expression for integer v is determined by starts. For composition H=R∘L:
- H(v)>=INF iff L(v)>=sat_R or L(v)>=INF (i.e., v>=sat_L). Since L increasing, first v is min(sat_L, first_ge(L, sat_R)). This is satH.
- For v<satH, L(v)<sat_R and L(v)<INF, so R unsaturated. Breakpoints of H occur at breakpoints of L and at preimages of breakpoints of R. We merge them. On each interval, both L and R are affine; compose coefficients. This is correct.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
from bisect import bisect_right

INF = 10**18
CONST_INF = (0, (), (), (), ())


def make_leaf(a, b):
    if b == 1:
        sat = INF - a
        starts = (0,)
        slopes = (1,)
        intercepts = (a,)
        val_ends = (INF - 1,)
        return (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, val_ends)

    # v + a >= b*v  <=>  v <= a / (b-1)
    start_mult = (a + b - 2) // (b - 1)

    sat_add = INF - a
    sat_mul = (INF + b - 1) // b
    sat = sat_add if sat_add < sat_mul else sat_mul

    starts = [0]
    slopes = [1]
    intercepts = [a]

    if start_mult < sat:
        starts.append(start_mult)
        slopes.append(b)
        intercepts.append(0)

    starts = tuple(starts)
    slopes = tuple(slopes)
    intercepts = tuple(intercepts)

    val_ends = []
    m = len(starts)
    for i in range(m):
        if i + 1 < m:
            end = starts[i + 1] - 1
        else:
            end = sat - 1
        val_ends.append(slopes[i] * end + intercepts[i])

    return (sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, tuple(val_ends))


def compose(L, R):
    if L is None:
        return R
    if R is None:
        return L

    satL, startsL, slopesL, interceptsL, val_endsL = L
    satR, startsR, slopesR, interceptsR, val_endsR = R

    if satL == 0 or satR == 0:
        return CONST_INF

    mL = len(startsL)
    L0 = interceptsL[0]
    p = 0
    pre = []
    nR = len(startsR)

    # Preimages of R's breakpoints under L.
    for idx in range(1, nR):
        y = startsR[idx]
        if y <= L0:
            pre.append(0)
        else:
            while p < mL and val_endsL[p] < y:
                p += 1
            if p >= mL:
                pre.append(satL)
            else:
                s = slopesL[p]
                c = interceptsL[p]
                v = (y - c + s - 1) // s
                if v < startsL[p]:
                    v = startsL[p]
                pre.append(v)

    # First v where L(v) >= satR.
    y = satR
    if y <= L0:
        vR = 0
    else:
        while p < mL and val_endsL[p] < y:
            p += 1
        if p >= mL:
            vR = satL
        else:
            s = slopesL[p]
            c = interceptsL[p]
            vR = (y - c + s - 1) // s
            if vR < startsL[p]:
                vR = startsL[p]

    satH = satL if satL < vR else vR
    if satH == 0:
        return CONST_INF

    Lf = []
    for x in startsL:
        if x < satH:
            Lf.append(x)
        else:
            break

    pref = []
    for v in pre:
        if v < satH:
            pref.append(v)

    # Merge starts.
    H_starts = []
    i = j = 0
    lenLf = len(Lf)
    lenPref = len(pref)
    while i < lenLf or j < lenPref:
        if j >= lenPref or (i < lenLf and Lf[i] < pref[j]):
            x = Lf[i]
            i += 1
        elif i >= lenLf or pref[j] < Lf[i]:
            x = pref[j]
            j += 1
        else:
            x = Lf[i]
            i += 1
            j += 1
        if not H_starts or H_starts[-1] != x:
            H_starts.append(x)

    H_slopes = []
    H_intercepts = []

    iL = 0
    iR = 0
    for v in H_starts:
        while iL + 1 < lenLf and Lf[iL + 1] <= v:
            iL += 1

        y = slopesL[iL] * v + interceptsL[iL]

        while iR + 1 < nR and startsR[iR + 1] <= y:
            iR += 1

        H_slopes.append(slopesR[iR] * slopesL[iL])
        H_intercepts.append(slopesR[iR] * interceptsL[iL] + interceptsR[iR])

    mH = len(H_starts)
    val_endsH = []
    for idx in range(mH):
        if idx + 1 < mH:
            end = H_starts[idx + 1] - 1
        else:
            end = satH - 1
        val_endsH.append(H_slopes[idx] * end + H_intercepts[idx])

    return (
        satH,
        tuple(H_starts),
        tuple(H_slopes),
        tuple(H_intercepts),
        tuple(val_endsH),
    )


def eval_node(node, v):
    if node is None:
        return v

    sat, starts, slopes, intercepts, _ = node
    if v >= sat:
        return INF

    idx = bisect_right(starts, v) - 1
    res = slopes[idx] * v + intercepts[idx]
    if res >= INF:
        return INF
    return res


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)

    N = next(it)
    A = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
    B = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]

    size = 1
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1

    tree = [None] * (2 * size)

    for i in range(N):
        tree[size + i] = make_leaf(A[i], B[i])

    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        tree[i] = compose(tree[i << 1], tree[i << 1 | 1])

    Q = next(it)
    out = []

    for _ in range(Q):
        t = next(it)

        if t == 1:
            i = next(it) - 1
            x = next(it)
            A[i] = x
            tree[size + i] = make_leaf(A[i], B[i])

            idx = (size + i) >> 1
            while idx:
                tree[idx] = compose(tree[idx << 1], tree[idx << 1 | 1])
                idx >>= 1

        elif t == 2:
            i = next(it) - 1
            x = next(it)
            B[i] = x
            tree[size + i] = make_leaf(A[i], B[i])

            idx = (size + i) >> 1
            while idx:
                tree[idx] = compose(tree[idx << 1], tree[idx << 1 | 1])
                idx >>= 1

        else:
            l = next(it) - 1
            r = next(it)  # exclusive

            l += size
            r += size

            left_nodes = []
            right_nodes = []

            while l < r:
                if l & 1:
                    left_nodes.append(tree[l])
                    l += 1
                if r & 1:
                    r -= 1
                    right_nodes.append(tree[r])
                l >>= 1
                r >>= 1

            v = 0
            for node in left_nodes + right_nodes[::-1]:
                v = eval_node(node, v)
                if v >= INF:
                    break

            out.append(str(v))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
